### [MINOR] (cross-surface) A required REF whose target row was deleted counts as filled on the readiness screen and as missing in the report's own warnings

**Verdict:** PARTIAL

**Where.** frontend/lib/submissionReadiness.ts:9-11 (the identity claim); frontend/lib/designWorkshopStore.ts:2008 (`const filled = isFilled(row[field.key]);` inside `scoreStageData`); backend/app/services/stage_schema.py:1254 and :1283-1305; backend/app/services/report_builder.py:1870-1881, :1899, :2108; backend/app/services/design_workshops.py:1365 (`workshop_completeness`, no `ref_resolves`)

**What it does.** The divergence is real and the evidence holds: `stage_completeness` takes `ref_resolves` (stage_schema.py:1254) and `_counts_as_filled` returns False for a REF whose id no longer resolves (:1300-1305); exactly one caller supplies it — the report builder, for the completeness annexure (report_builder.py:1899) and for the per-stage `X-Report-Warnings` lines (:2108) — and `ReportBuilder.ref_resolves` (:1870-1881) is `bool(self._ref_label(value))`. The web's `scoreStageData` judges every field, REF included, with `isFilled` (designWorkshopStore.ts:2008 — the finder cited :1698, which is inside `foldStageInto`'s docstring). So `submissionReadiness.ts:9-11`'s claim that its blocking set is 'the very list the report already prints in X-Report-Warnings' is false for a dangling REF. But the finder's mechanism, consequence and remedy are all wrong in the same direction, and the correction matters: `workshop_completeness` (design_workshops.py:1365), which is what `GET /{id}` returns (routes:987) and what every non-report scorer uses, ALSO omits `ref_resolves`. The web therefore agrees with the server about submittability; the odd one out is the report builder, deliberately so — the docstring at stage_schema.py:1292-1298 says the argument 'is what the REPORT passes' because 'the stage form scores itself on the phone with no database'. The screen's sentence 'Nothing on this workshop will be refused for a missing Basic field' is not contradicted by anything. What IS defective is narrower: `reportChecks` (submissionReadiness.ts:290-375) raises only the two stage-20 checks, so the readiness screen's 'What the report would warn about' section omits warnings the report will actually emit, and the module header asserts a guarantee the code does not keep.

**How it fails.** A designer deletes a stage-13 prototype row that eighteen rows across stages 14-17 reference through the required `prototypeRef` (stage_definitions.py:1090, `f("prototypeRef", "Prototype", REF, B, required=True, ref_model="DwPrototype")`; the same shape at :993 `sketchRef` and :1044 `artisanRef`). The readiness screen counts all eighteen filled and lists nothing under 'What the report would warn about'; the generated .docx then carries four 'Stage 15 (…): 1 required field(s) not recorded' warning lines, scores those stages below 100% in the completeness annexure, and prints 'Prototype | Not recorded.' in the tables.

**What it costs.** The readiness screen's second section under-reports what the report will warn about, and its header states an identity with `X-Report-Warnings` that a maintainer will trust and that does not hold. No submission is wrongly permitted and no data is lost — the report itself is correct and says so.

**Remedy.** Not the finder's remedy. Adding a client-side `refResolves` to `scoreStageData` would make the browser disagree with `workshop_completeness` and with the phone, which is the third-opinion failure that module's header forbids. Either (a) correct the header at submissionReadiness.ts:9-11 to state the one exception, and add a dangling-REF entry to `reportChecks` as a CHECK (report-warning class, not a blocker) built from the draft — every one of these refs points inside the workshop the draft already holds, so `workshopCodeMatchesRow`-style matching on `_entryId`/`_clientKey` answers it offline; or (b) give `workshop_completeness` the same `ref_resolves` argument so the two servers agree first and the clients can then be ported to one rule.

**What the verifier checked.** I tried to kill this by finding a second caller of `stage_completeness` that passes `ref_resolves` — `grep ref_resolves` over app/services and app/api/routes returns only stage_schema's own definition and report_builder's two call sites plus one comment in custom_sections.py:169 ('NO REF, because ref_resolves is supplied by the REPORT and by nothing else'), which confirms the asymmetry is deliberate and documented. That is what demoted the finding: the finder framed the web as diverging from the report while the SERVER's own workshop-level scorer diverges identically, so this is not a client defect and the readiness screen is not wrong about the submit gate — I looked for a completeness-based submit refusal in routes/design_workshops.py and found none, so 'a submit is refused while any of these is empty' rests on `workshop_completeness`, the ref-blind scorer. I verified three of the twelve cited required REF fields verbatim (stage_definitions.py:993, 1044, 1090) rather than taking the list on trust. Severity dropped from MAJOR to MINOR: no wrong data reaches a document, no answer is lost, and the report is the surface that gets it right.

## LOW — 29

### [LOW] (backend) The in-process audio conversion's memory bound reads `MediaFile.sizeBytes`, a number the client declares at `/media/complete` and the server never reconciles against the stored object

**Verdict:** PARTIAL

**Where.** backend/app/api/routes/data_browser.py:2970-2986 (download_media's 413 and the read), backend/app/schemas/media.py:102, backend/app/api/routes/media.py:507, backend/app/services/s3.py:175-199 (presign_put_url)

**What it does.** Confirmed as quoted. `size = int(media.sizeBytes or 0)` guards a 200 MiB ceiling (`MAX_CONVERT_BYTES`, data_browser.py:141) before `get_object_bytes` pulls the whole object into the heap and `_convert_audio_to_mp4` (pydub/ffmpeg) decodes it into a second copy. `sizeBytes: int = Field(gt=0)` (schemas/media.py:102) has a lower bound only, is stored verbatim through `clean_data(payload.model_dump(...))` (media.py:507), and no `head_object` exists anywhere in `backend/app` — grepped. `presign_put_url` (s3.py:190-199) signs Bucket/Key/ContentType with no `content-length-range` condition, so the object's real size is unconstrained by anything the server controls.

WHERE THE FINDER IS WRONG. (a) The 'row written before the column was populated, where `int(None or 0)` is 0' half is refuted: `MediaFile.sizeBytes` is `BigInt` NOT NULL (schema.prisma:789) and the request field is required with `gt=0`, so there is no null-sizeBytes row this route can meet. The `or 0` is dead defensive code, not a live hole. (b) The failure scenario's 'any dataset-downloader who later clicks that row' is wrong: `download_media` first narrows by `scope.media` (data_browser.py:2949-2955), so a below-Professor caller can only reach their OWN or granted uploads. The attacker has to trigger the OOM on their own file, or be Professor+, or wait for a Professor+ to click it.

**How it fails.** An account holding `canDownloadDataset` (required by `require_dataset_downloader`, deps.py:473-479) presigns an upload declaring `sizeBytes: 1024`, PUTs a 1.5 GB body to the returned URL — nothing in the SigV4 signature bounds the body — and calls `/media/complete` with `mediaType: "AUDIO"`. They then request `/api/data/media/{id}/download` on their own row. The 413 does not fire because the column says 1 KB; `get_object_bytes` pulls 1.5 GB into the heap of the single-worker uvicorn process and pydub/ffmpeg decodes it into a second copy. The box OOMs, taking every in-flight request with it. The non-malicious variant is a client that computes `sizeBytes` wrongly for a large recording and a Professor who clicks it.

**What it costs.** The one memory bound on the heaviest synchronous path in the web process is set by the caller rather than by the server. It is a self-inflicted denial of service available to any account already trusted with the dataset-download permission, not an unprivileged one.

**Remedy.** Do not trust the column for a safety decision. Add `s3.object_size(key)` beside `get_object_bytes` (a `head_object` `ContentLength`), call it under `asyncio.to_thread` in `download_media`, and compare THAT against `MAX_CONVERT_BYTES` — one cheap round trip on a path about to spend an ffmpeg transcode. Separately, reconcile the declared size at `/media/complete`: one `head_object` per completed upload, storing the true `ContentLength` (or 422 on mismatch), so the number the browser prints as 'size' is a fact. Do NOT add a `content-length-range` condition to `presign_put_url` — s3.py:178-188 records that adding signed conditions there breaks every installed Android build, and the same trap applies.

**What the verifier checked.** Read data_browser.py:2927-3013, schemas/media.py:98-132, media.py:482-530, s3.py:143-199. Tried to kill it four ways. (1) Is there a HeadObject anywhere? Grepped `head_object` across backend/app — none. (2) Does the presign bound the body? Read `presign_put_url` — Params are Bucket/Key/ContentType only. (3) Is `sizeBytes` validated anywhere else? Grepped all uses: stored at media.py:507, printed at data_browser.py ~506 and ~2530, part arithmetic at media.py:189, and this bound. Nowhere reconciled. (4) Who can reach the route? `require_dataset_downloader` (deps.py:473-479) plus `scope.media` narrowing — which kills the finder's 'any dataset-downloader clicks that row' framing and is why I dropped MINOR to LOW. Also refuted the null-sizeBytes half against schema.prisma:789 (`sizeBytes BigInt`, not nullable). Checked docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md — not recorded.

### [LOW] (backend) `POST /report` accepts a two-format request its own schema documents as the common case and silently renders only the first, recording only the first in the export ledger

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED

**Where.** backend/app/api/routes/design_workshops.py:2770 (`fmt = payload.formats[0]`), :2783-2812 (one render, one response, one export row); backend/app/schemas/design_workshops.py:257-266 and :329-339

```python
# schemas/design_workshops.py:260-262
    ``format`` is a list so the common case — "give me both, I am about to submit them" — is one
    request and one consistent snapshot of the data, rather than two requests that could
    straddle an edit.

# schemas/design_workshops.py:338 — a two-element list validates and is sorted
        self.formats = sorted(wanted)

# routes/design_workshops.py:2770 — only the first is ever used
    fmt = payload.formats[0]
```

**What it does.** Verified exactly as described. `_known_formats` (:329-339) rejects only unknown tokens and an empty list, then normalises to a sorted list, so `{"formats": ["DOCX","PDF"]}` validates to `["DOCX","PDF"]`. The handler takes `[0]`, renders once (:2783-2785), builds one filename and one content-disposition (:2788-2793), and writes one `DwReportExport` row with `format: fmt` (:2802-2806). Because the list is sorted, a request for both always yields the DOCX and drops the PDF. Nothing in the response body, the `x-report-warnings` header or the ledger says a requested format was dropped. The route's own docstring says "Render and return one report file" (:2758) — so the ROUTE is honest and the SCHEMA docstring is the thing that is wrong.

**How it fails.** Any client written against the schema — an integration, a script, or the export page the moment somebody adds a "give me both" checkbox — posts both formats, gets HTTP 200 with a .docx whose content-type and filename are indistinguishable from a single-format request, and files it believing the PDF came too. I confirmed the door is held shut only by callers: the web sends `formats: [format]` (frontend/app/(protected)/design-workshops/[id]/report/page.tsx:501) and `DwReportRequest.formats` is typed as an array (frontend/lib/designWorkshops.ts:659); Android does not call this endpoint with a formats list at all; every backend test sends `["DOCX"]`. So it is latent, which is why LOW is the honest severity.

**What it costs.** A documented API capability silently does half of what its docstring says, and the dropped half is undetectable from the response. The export ledger records one DOCX at that timestamp, so the omission is invisible in the audit trail too.

**Remedy.** Refusing is the smaller and more honest change: add `if len(wanted) > 1: raise ValueError("ask for one format per request")` to `_known_formats` (schemas/design_workshops.py:329-339) and rewrite the class docstring at :258-263, which currently promises the opposite — also fixing the stale name, since it says ``format`` and the field is `formats`. Honouring it instead means rendering each format, returning a zip or multipart body, and writing one `DwReportExport` row per file; that changes the content-type contract for every existing caller and should not be done to fix a docstring.

**What the verifier checked.** Read routes/design_workshops.py:2753-2812 and schemas/design_workshops.py:257-339 in full; every quote is verbatim. Tried to refute: (a) looked for a second render or a loop over `payload.formats` anywhere in the handler — there is none, one `render_report` call at :2783 and one `Response`; (b) looked for a validator rejecting multi-element lists — `_known_formats` explicitly permits them and only rejects empty and unknown; (c) checked whether `sorted` could ever put PDF first — `sorted({"DOCX","PDF"})` is `["DOCX","PDF"]`, so PDF is always the casualty; (d) checked every caller in the repo (web page.tsx:501, lib/designWorkshops.ts:659 and :1526, all backend tests) — all send exactly one format, which is what keeps this latent and caps it at LOW. Not in docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md. The finder's severity and remedy are both right; I am confirming as written.

---

### [LOW] (backend) `POST /{id}/report` renders only `formats[0]` while `ReportGenerateIn` documents a multi-format request, so a client asking for both files silently receives one

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED

**Where.** backend/app/api/routes/design_workshops.py:2753-2817 (`fmt = payload.formats[0]` at :2770, export row at :2803-2816, single `Response` at :2817); backend/app/schemas/design_workshops.py:257-266 and :329-339

**What it does.** Verified as quoted. The docstring on `ReportGenerateIn` says the list exists so that "give me both, I am about to submit them" is one request and one consistent snapshot. The route takes `fmt = payload.formats[0]`, renders once, builds one `content-disposition`, writes at most one `DwReportExport` row with `"format": fmt`, and returns a single `Response`. `_known_formats` (schemas:329-339) rewrites the list as `sorted(wanted)`, so `["PDF","DOCX"]` normalises to `["DOCX","PDF"]` and the survivor is always the DOCX. Nothing about the dropped format is added to `x-report-warnings`, and `x-report-warning-count` is `str(len(warnings))` from the builder alone.

**How it fails.** A client following the documented shape posts `{"formats": ["DOCX","PDF"], "record": true}` and gets HTTP 200 with one .docx, one export row, and no header saying the PDF was never made. I confirmed no shipped client does this: the only browser sender is `frontend/app/(protected)/design-workshops/[id]/report/page.tsx:501` with `formats: [format]`, and `frontend/lib/designWorkshops.ts:1526` reads `body.formats[0]` for the fallback file name — the web is single-format on both ends. Every e2e and backend test I found sends one element (`report-download.spec.ts:49`, `report-accent.spec.ts:67`, `test_stage_sync.py:341,356`, `test_questionnaire_forms.py:1223+`). The handset renders on device.

**What it costs.** A documented request shape that cannot be honoured and does not say so; and because the export row is written from the surviving format alone, the history the office reads via `GET /exports` and `reportDiff.identicalFile` would silently record one file for a request that asked for two.

**Remedy.** Narrow the contract to what the code does — `formats: list[str] = Field(default_factory=…, max_length=1)` or a validator that refuses more than one with a message naming the two requests to make — so a caller asking for both gets a 422 it can act on rather than a 200 that loses half the answer. Honouring the promise instead means a container response (zip or two base64 blobs) plus two `DwReportExport` rows from the one snapshot.

**What the verifier checked.** I tried to refute it as taste rather than a defect, and as already-guarded. Guard: I read `_known_formats` in full — it rejects unknown tokens and empty lists, but nothing caps the length, so a two-element body validates and 200s. Taste: it is not naming or style; it is a documented request shape that is half-executed with no signal, which is the contract-mismatch band. I also checked whether the route might loop or zip further down — it does not; there is exactly one `render_report` call, one export create and one `Response`. Not in docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md. LOW confirmed and correctly justified by the finder: no shipped client sends two formats today, so it is latent.

---

### [LOW] (backend) `/designers/directory` drops suspended designers AFTER `take=500`, so the cap applies to the wrong set — latent, because both shipped callers disable the filter

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED

**Where.** backend/app/api/routes/designers.py:242 and :262-265; against backend/app/services/design_workshop_viewers.py's own rule for `eligible_viewers`

**What it does.** Verified. Line 242 is `users = await db.user.find_many(where=where, order={"name": "asc"}, take=500)`; the roster is read afterwards (:249-252) and the `continue` at :265 drops rows the `take` has already spent, so the answer is an arbitrary number below 500 with eligible accounts beyond the cut never considered. The `search` term IS folded into the same WHERE (:236-241), so only the suspension filter is post-take. There is no `truncated` on the wire; both clients infer it from length (`DIRECTORY_CAP` at frontend/app/(protected)/admin/designers/page.tsx:75 and :147, `DESIGNER_DIRECTORY_CAP = 500` at android/…/DesignerRosterScreen.kt:471), and that inference is exactly what a post-take drop breaks. Latent today because every shipped caller passes `includeSuspended: true` — page.tsx:143 and android WorkshopRepository.kt:826-827 — which makes the `continue` unreachable. The defective arm is the route's own default.

**How it fails.** Any future caller that omits `includeSuspended` (the default, and the arm the docstring at :225-229 describes as the useful one) on a deployment with more than 500 workshop-capable accounts gets a short list: twenty suspended designers sorting inside the first 500 come back as 480 rows, the clients' `>= 500` check reads false, and the screen reports a complete list that is missing every eligible designer past the cut.

**What it costs.** The completeness signal is wrong in the one direction that matters — "complete" about a list that is not — and it fails on the default parameters. Same class as the eligible-viewers defect closed on 2026-08-13, in the sibling endpoint. LOW because no shipped caller can reach it.

**Remedy.** Fold the roster into the user query's WHERE the way `eligible_viewers` does: read the active roster emails first and, when `includeSuspended` is false, compose `{"OR": [{"role": {"in": [ADMIN, MASTER_ADMIN]}}, {"AND": [{"role": "DESIGNER"}, {"email": {"in": admitted, "mode": "insensitive"}}]}]}` — insensitive, for the reason recorded in the closed roster-fold entry — so the cap applies to rows that are already eligible. Take LIMIT+1 and return an explicit `truncated`, rather than leaving two clients to infer it from a length.

**What the verifier checked.** Read designers.py:218-286 in full: `take=500` at :242, the roster/profile reads at :249-252, and the `continue` at :265 are exactly as described, and the search is correctly inside the WHERE. Checked whether it is reachable by a real user: grepped every caller — frontend page.tsx:143 `listDesignerDirectory({ includeSuspended: true })` and android WorkshopRepositoryApi.kt:1018-1020 / WorkshopRepository.kt:827, both hard-coded true — so no live path takes the defective arm. Verified the clients' length-based truncation constants at page.tsx:75/147 and DesignerRosterScreen.kt:471. Confirmed the finder's own LOW severity and 'latent today' framing is accurate; I checked specifically whether the `>= 500` inference could still misreport on the includeSuspended=true arm, and it cannot — with the filter disabled the returned length equals what the take produced.

---

### [LOW] (backend) `/export/dataset`'s seven capped table reads have no ORDER BY, so which rows fall inside the cap is Postgres's arbitrary choice

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED

**Where.** backend/app/api/routes/export.py:153-174 (six record reads) and :225 (the media read); contrast :396-419 (the two CSV routes)

**What it does.** Exact. Seven `find_many` calls carry `take` (`EXPORT_TAKE = 5000`, `MEDIA_TAKE = 20000`) and no `order`: workshop, artisan, productdocumentation, tooldocumentation, questionnaireinterview, process, mediafile. A LIMIT with no ORDER BY has no defined row set in Postgres; the surviving rows can change with statistics, with a concurrent vacuum, or with an index choice. The two CSV routes in the same file get it right (`order={"createdAt": "desc"}` at :403 and :416), so the omission is local to the manifest. This is a distinct instance from the closed viewer-picker finding — different module, different cap — and that entry (docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md, 'Closed on 2026-08-13', item 1 of the mutation-testing entry) states the lesson verbatim: 'with no ORDER BY the cut is non-deterministic: two identical requests hide two different populations'.

**How it fails.** The archive crosses 5000 rows in any one of the six tables (or 20000 media rows). A researcher downloads the repository zip on Monday for a submission and again on Friday after a reviewer asks for a corrected copy. The two zips can contain different rows — not a superset, an arbitrary re-draw — and both report `truncated: true` with nothing naming which 5000 they hold. A reviewer diffing the two concludes records were deleted. Reachability is the honest limit: the closed OPEN_FINDINGS entries measure 731 artisans and ~925 media on the live corpus, so the caps are nowhere near being hit today and this is latent.

**What it costs.** A truncated archival export is not reproducible. `truncated: true` tells a researcher the set is short but not that it is also unstable, which is the more damaging half — and it is the half no client can compensate for.

**Remedy.** Add a total order to all seven reads: `order=[{"createdAt": "asc"}, {"id": "asc"}]`. A timestamp alone is not unique (the closed picker finding measured 204 accounts sharing one sort key, docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md), so the id tiebreaker is load-bearing. `createdAt` ascending rather than descending so successive exports are nested rather than disjoint as the archive grows. Then return the capped table names alongside `truncated` instead of a bare boolean, so a researcher can tell which population was cut.

**What the verifier checked.** Read export.py:153-226 and :396-419. Tried to kill it three ways. (1) Is an order applied elsewhere — a default on the delegate, or a sort after the read? No: nothing sorts `workshops`/`artisans`/etc. before the emit loops, and Prisma applies no implicit order. (2) Does `truncated` at least make it self-evident? It flags shortness, not instability, and is a single boolean over all seven reads. (3) Is it already recorded? The ORDER BY lesson appears in OPEN_FINDINGS.md but attached to `design_workshop_viewers.eligible_viewers`, which is CLOSED and is a different module and cap — so this is a distinct, unrecorded instance and not a duplicate. LOW confirmed and left at the finder's severity: the caps are an order of magnitude above the measured corpus, so it needs the archive to grow first.

---

### [LOW] (backend) GET /feedback reads the whole Feedback table with no take, no skip and no page parameters, joining every author row

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED

**Where.** backend/app/api/routes/feedback.py:81-88 (`list_feedback`), with the per-row text bounds at backend/app/schemas/feedback.py:20-25 (five columns at `max_length=5000`)

**What it does.** `db.feedback.find_many(order=[{"updatedAt": "desc"}], include={"user": True})` with no `take`, no `skip` and no query parameters at all — the route signature is `async def list_feedback(_: Any = Depends(require_master_admin))`. Every row is serialised in memory and returned as one array. The table holds at most one row per account that has ever given feedback (`PUT /feedback/me` upserts on `userId`), and this repository has 3632 accounts per its own measurement in docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md. Sibling list routes in the same domain (`/tasks`, `/tasks/batches`, `/artisans`) all take `page`/`pageSize` with `le=100`.

**How it fails.** After a pilot in which a large share of accounts answered the five free-text prompts in detail, the master admin opens the feedback page. `GET /feedback` assembles every row plus its joined User in one response with no ceiling, and there is no smaller request the page can make instead. The read only ever grows, and its first failure signal is a timeout.

**What it costs.** Narrow and today harmless — one master-admin screen over a table bounded by the account count, with realistic bodies far below the 25 KB per-row worst case. But it is an uncapped read of a monotonically growing table with no failure signal, which is the exact shape `ACTIVE_ROSTER_READ_LIMIT`'s own rationale (docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md, closed 2026-08-13) argues against leaving in place.

**Remedy.** Give it the pagination every sibling has: `page: int = Query(1, ge=1)`, `pageSize: int = Query(20, ge=1, le=100)`, `normalize_pagination`, `count_and_page`, returning `page_payload`. Add `{"id": "desc"}` as a tiebreaker to the `updatedAt` order so paging cannot repeat or skip a row when two feedbacks share an update instant. `frontend/app/(protected)/feedback/page.tsx:211` fetches `Feedback[]` and would need to read the paged envelope in the same change.

**What the verifier checked.** Read feedback.py end to end (89 lines) and schemas/feedback.py end to end; the route is exactly as quoted and there is genuinely no bound. I looked for a rescue: no reverse proxy limit is expressed in the codebase for this route, `_serialize` does not truncate the text columns, and the client (frontend/app/(protected)/feedback/page.tsx:211) requests the whole array unconditionally with no parameters, so nothing narrows it. I also checked the neighbouring write path for a worse defect while I was there — `upsert_my_feedback` writes all thirteen columns from the payload, so a partial PUT would null the rest — and refuted it as reachable: both clients send the whole form seeded from a prior GET (frontend page.tsx:242-246; MainActivity.kt:8990-9013 seeds every field from `myFeedback()` before MainActivity.kt:9072-9086 sends all thirteen). Severity LOW retained.

---

### [LOW] (backend) batchTimezone is the only field on PUT /settings with no validation, and an unusable value is swallowed so the off-peak window runs in IST while the settings page displays something else

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED

**Where.** backend/app/api/routes/settings.py:44-92 (`update_app_settings`: the mode check at 51-55, the two HH:mm checks at 56-61, the provider-order check at 62-68, and nothing for batchTimezone), backend/app/schemas/settings.py:42 (`batchTimezone: str | None = None`), backend/app/services/app_settings.py:204-228 (`within_processing_window`, the swallow at 213-220), frontend/components/settings/AppSettingsPanel.tsx:173-181 (a free-text input)

**What it does.** Every sibling field on the body is validated — `transcriptionMode` against `VALID_TRANSCRIPTION_MODES`, `batchWindowStart`/`batchWindowEnd` through `is_valid_hhmm`, `sttProviderOrder` through `invalid_stt_provider_order` — and `batchTimezone` is written straight to the row. `within_processing_window` then does `try: tz = ZoneInfo(tz_name)` / `except Exception: … ZoneInfo(DEFAULT_TIMEZONE)` / `except Exception: timezone(timedelta(hours=5, minutes=30), name="IST")` with no log line and no signal to any caller. The stored string is echoed back by `AppSettingDto` (schemas/settings.py:24), so the page redraws showing whatever was typed. The field is genuinely free text on the web: `<input type="text" … onChange={(event) => setSettings({ ...settings, batchTimezone: event.target.value })}` with a placeholder rather than a picker.

**How it fails.** A master admin sets the window to 22:00–05:00 and types the zone as "IST", or "India Standard Time", or an alias the host's tzdata does not carry. The PUT returns 200 and the page redraws with exactly what they typed. The queue evaluates the window in Asia/Kolkata regardless. On a deployment whose intended zone is not IST, heavy transcription runs at the wrong hours and competes with the daytime uploads the window exists to protect, while the settings screen displays a zone the queue is not using.

**What it costs.** A settings write can leave the row describing something other than what happens, on the one page whose purpose is to say when the servers do their heavy work. It cannot wedge anything — the fallback chain is safe and terminates — and the deployment's real zone is Asia/Kolkata, which is also the fallback, so on this deployment the wrong-hours outcome needs the admin to have wanted a non-IST zone. LOW is right.

**Remedy.** Add an `is_valid_timezone(value: str) -> bool` beside `is_valid_hhmm` in `app_settings` (which already imports `ZoneInfo`) and check it in `update_app_settings` next to the two HH:mm checks, 422-ing with the offending value named. On a host with no tz database at all, accept only `DEFAULT_TIMEZONE` rather than accepting everything. Independently, `within_processing_window` should log at WARNING when it falls back, so a value that slipped in before the check existed is visible in the journal instead of silent.

**What the verifier checked.** Read settings.py:44-92 and app_settings.py:184-228 verbatim; the quotes are exact and the absence of a batchTimezone branch is real. I checked the schema for a hidden constraint (schemas/settings.py:42 is a bare `str | None = None`; the response model at :24 is a bare `str`) and the database for one (prisma/schema.prisma:1266 is `String @default("Asia/Kolkata")`, no enum, no check constraint). I specifically checked whether the client makes the value unreachable — a dropdown of IANA zones would have killed it — and AppSettingsPanel.tsx:173-181 is a plain text input, so an arbitrary string reaches the API through the supported UI. Android sends the field too (ApiModels.kt:1375). Severity LOW retained, matching the finder.

### [LOW] (backend) An `entryId` is matched without checking it belongs to the entity that sent it, and the update never rewrites `entityKey` — so a mismatched id writes one entity's data into another's row and lets the sweep delete the right one

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED

**Where.** backend/app/services/design_workshops.py:903 (`by_id = {row.id: row for row in live}`), :1014-1016 (the match), :1040 (`touched_ids.add(row.id)`), :1102-1105 (the update, which writes only `data`, `ordinal`, `deletedAt`)

**What it does.** Exact as quoted. `by_id` is keyed by row id alone over every live row of the stage, across all its entities. The clientKey lookup two lines below is correctly keyed by `(entity.key, client_key)` (:1019) and the singleton fallback is correctly scoped by `r.entityKey == entity.key` (:1031-1032), so this is the one arm of the three-way matcher with no entity guard. Nothing downstream recovers: the UPDATE does not write `entityKey`, so the row keeps its original entity while its `data` becomes the other entity's cleaned values; `touched_ids` gains the wrong id, so the row that should have been updated is untouched and is soft-deleted by the sweep (:1189-1197) as a row the payload no longer holds. `validate_entry` cannot notice, because it validates against the entity the PAYLOAD named.

**How it fails.** Stage 5 declares three collections (`processStep`, `tool`, `rawMaterial`). Any caller that lets an `_entryId` cross collections — a bulk import, a repair script, a draft migration that re-keys rows, or a hand-made PUT from an account with edit rights — sends a `tool` entry carrying a `processStep` row's id. The server updates the `processStep` row's `data` with tool values while it remains `entityKey = "processStep"`, and the real `tool` row, named by nothing in `touched_ids`, is soft-deleted by the sweep. The response reads `saved: 1, removed: 1`.

**What it costs.** One row of workshop data is silently replaced by another entity's answers and a second is deleted, under one 200; the corrupted row then renders in the process-step table of the report with a tool's fields. LOW is right — no shipped client mixes entryIds across collections (the web lifts `_entryId` per collection in `buildStageEntries`, the phone the same), so this needs a bespoke caller — but the guard is free and the outcome is unrecoverable.

**Remedy.** One condition at the match site: `row = by_id.get(entry.entryId); if row is not None and row.entityKey != entity.key: dropped.append(f"{entity.key}._entryId={entry.entryId} (belongs to {row.entityKey})"); row = None`. Falling through to the clientKey lookup is the right recovery — the row is then matched by its real identity or created — and reporting it in `dropped` gives the same visibility the duplicate-clientKey collision two lines below already gets.

**What the verifier checked.** Read the whole matcher (:1014-1032) and the write (:1095-1115). Looked specifically for a guard: the clientKey arm and the singleton arm are both entity-scoped, the entryId arm is not, and no later statement re-checks or rewrites `entityKey`. Confirmed `by_id` spans the stage's rows for all entities (`live` is filtered only on `deletedAt`) and that an id from a different STAGE cannot match, because the read at :895-897 is scoped by stageKey — so the blast radius is one stage. Checked both clients for a path that could emit a cross-entity id and found none, which is why LOW stands. Not in docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md.

---

### [LOW] (backend) Two entries carrying the same `entryId` in one payload both write to the same row — the first entry's answers are silently overwritten and `saved` counts them both — while the identical collision on `_clientKey` is guarded three lines away

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED · found by the verifier, not the original audit

**Where.** backend/app/services/design_workshops.py:1014-1016 (the entryId match, unguarded) against :1020-1027 (`claimed_client_keys`, the guard for the same collision on the other key), and :1095-1105 with :1204-1205 (two update tuples for one row id, applied in order)

**What it does.** `claimed_client_keys` exists because "two rows cannot share one client key — the unique index says so" and letting a duplicate through "produced a raw 500". The same duplication on `entryId` has no guard and no index to catch it: both entries resolve to the same row through `by_id`, both are appended to `pending` with the same `row_id`, both become `(row_id, {...})` update tuples, and the transaction applies them in order so the SECOND entry's data wins wholesale. Neither entry creates a row, `dropped` gains nothing, `errors` gains nothing, and the response reports `saved: 2, updated: 2` for one surviving row. `touched_ids` also protects that row from the sweep, so no collateral deletion makes the loss visible.

**How it fails.** Any client or script that copies a row's `data` to make a second row — the data carries `_entryId`, which is put there by the stage read (design_workshops.py:1447) and by `assemble_workshop_data` — so a bulk import, a draft repair, or a future "duplicate this row" affordance sends two entries with one id. Two rows go up, one row comes back, and the response says two were saved.

**What it costs.** One row of a designer's answers is destroyed under a 200 that reports success for both. The asymmetry with the clientKey guard three lines away is what makes this a defect rather than an accepted limit: the module already decided that a client sending one identity twice must be reported rather than obeyed. LOW because no shipped client duplicates entryIds today — neither the web nor the phone offers a row-copy affordance (grepped both).

**Remedy.** Give `entryId` the same treatment as `_clientKey`: keep a `claimed_row_ids: set[str]`, and when `by_id.get(entry.entryId)` returns a row already claimed by an earlier entry of this payload, append `f"{entity.key}._entryId={entry.entryId} (duplicate in payload)"` to `dropped` and set `row = None` so the entry falls through to the clientKey match or becomes a create. One set and three lines, beside the guard it mirrors.

### [LOW] (backend) The Settings hub shows the STORED key's hint beside the ENVIRONMENT key's value when an override cannot be decrypted, so the fingerprint an admin uses to tell two keys apart names the wrong one

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED · found by the verifier, not the original audit

**Where.** backend/app/services/managed_secrets.py:630 — `"hint": (row.hint if stored else _hint(environment))` inside `_describe`, with `stored = row is not None` at :606 and the hint's stated purpose at :590-595

**What it does.** `hint` is chosen by row existence, exactly like `source` is. When `refresh_if_stale` could not decrypt the row (:435-438) the value actually in force is the environment key, but `_describe` returns `row.hint` — the last four characters of the STORED, unreadable key — while `get_secret`, every provider call and `GET /secrets/{key}/reveal` all use the environment value. The hint's own docstring says it exists "so an admin can tell two keys apart without revealing either"; in this state it identifies the key that is NOT being used. This is a third surface of the same root cause as the two secrets findings above (provenance derived from row existence rather than from which value resolved), on a different field and a different UI element, which is why it is listed separately rather than folded into either.

**How it fails.** After a JWT_SECRET rotation with no explicit SECRETS_ENCRYPTION_KEY, the master admin compares the ElevenLabs hint on the Settings hub against the last four characters of the key in the provider dashboard to work out which key is live. The hint matches the override they pasted last month; the key actually being sent to the provider is the deployment's environment variable, whose last four characters are different and are never shown.

**What it costs.** The one non-revealing identifier on the settings list points at a key that is not in force, so an operator reconciling keys against a provider dashboard reaches the wrong conclusion about which credential the deployment is using. Low: the row's `lastStatus` is simultaneously FAILED with the undecryptable message (unless `test_secret` has just cleared it — see the sibling finding), so a careful reader has a contradicting signal on the same row.

**Remedy.** Have `_describe` take the resolved value rather than infer it: pass in whether the override actually decrypted (`key in _overrides` after `refresh_if_stale`) and derive `source`, `hint` and `configured` from that one fact. Then `hint` names the environment key whenever the environment key is what will be sent, and a `database-unreadable` provenance can carry the stored hint separately if it is still wanted for identification.

### [LOW] (backend) The rate-limit cooldown is per-process module state, but `POST /media/jobs/process` runs in the web process while the drain runs in a separate systemd worker — so the backoff does not hold, and a stale-recovered job can be claimed twice

**Verdict:** PARTIAL

**Where.** backend/app/services/media_queue.py:49-51 (the comment and the globals), :73-90, :286-335, :461-478 (recover_stale_processing_jobs), :481-502 (_lock_job), backend/app/api/routes/media.py:919-924, backend/app/main.py:131-139, backend/app/worker.py:1-35, backend/Dockerfile:267-273, backend/DEPLOY_AWS.md:96-97

**What it does.** The conclusion holds but THE FINDER'S MECHANISM IS WRONG, and the truth is simpler and stronger. There is no multi-worker uvicorn: Dockerfile:273 pins `--workers 1` with a comment (`:267-272`) explaining that `--workers 2` caused a production outage. The election in main.py:131-139 is therefore not what separates the two processes. What separates them is that the queue drain does not run in the web process at all: `app/worker.py` is a standalone systemd unit (`fieldrepo-queue.service`) and the web box sets `MEDIA_QUEUE_WORKER_ENABLED=false` (DEPLOY_AWS.md:96-97, :211; backend/.env.example:167 'false on the web box'). So `POST /media/jobs/process` (media.py:919-924) executes `process_next_media_jobs` inside the uvicorn process, while `_rate_limit_cooldown_until` and `_consecutive_rate_limits` that the drain maintains live in the queue process's memory. Two separate address spaces, always — not sometimes. The comment at media_queue.py:49-51, 'Single elected worker (see main.py), so module-level cooldown state is safe', is wrong on both clauses: the election is not what runs the drain in production, and the state is not shared with the one route that also drains.

The second half is confirmed as described: `recover_stale_processing_jobs` (:461-478) decides a job is dead purely from `lockedAt` age against a 30-minute cutoff, with no liveness check on `lockedBy`, and `process_next_media_jobs` calls it unconditionally at :293 — including on the manual-API path. `_lock_job`'s CAS (:489-501) defends against two claimers of a QUEUED row, not against a PROCESSING row being reset underneath its live holder.

**How it fails.** The transcription provider starts 429-ing overnight. The queue process enters a cooldown of up to 900s (`_enter_rate_limit_cooldown`, :77-84). An admin, seeing the queue grow, presses 'Run queue now' in the media-jobs panel; that request runs in the uvicorn process, whose `_rate_limit_cooldown_until` is `None`, so `allow_transcription` is true (:300-303) and it selects TRANSCRIPTION jobs and re-hits the throttled provider. Separately, a clip whose provider call has hung past 30 minutes is flipped back to QUEUED by that same manual pass's `recover_stale_processing_jobs`, then honestly re-locked and re-sent while the queue process is still awaiting the first answer — the same recording transcribed and billed twice, with the two results racing to write `MediaFile.transcriptText`.

**What it costs.** The backoff that exists to stop the deployment being blocked by its provider is defeated by the exact button an operator presses when the queue looks stuck, and the queue's double-run defence has a hole that only opens under the condition it was written for. Narrow in practice: it needs an active 429 storm or a >30-minute hung provider call plus an admin pressing the button.

**Remedy.** Move the cooldown out of module globals into state the whole host can see — an `AppSetting` key holding `transcriptionCooldownUntil` and the consecutive count — read at the top of `process_next_media_jobs` and written by `_enter_rate_limit_cooldown` / `_clear_rate_limit_cooldown`. Correct the comment at media_queue.py:49-51: it should say the drain runs in `app/worker.py`'s own process and that `POST /media/jobs/process` runs elsewhere, which is what made this look safe. For the recovery, the simplest correct fix is to have `POST /media/jobs/process` skip `recover_stale_processing_jobs` entirely (a `recover=False` parameter on `process_next_media_jobs`) and leave recovery to the queue process, which is serial by construction and cannot recover a job it is itself holding.

**What the verifier checked.** Read media_queue.py:38-90 and :286-335 and :461-502, media.py:919-924, main.py:115-169, worker.py:1-60. Tried to kill it four ways. (1) Are there really multiple uvicorn workers? NO — Dockerfile:273 is `--workers 1`, which kills the finder's stated mechanism outright. (2) Does that save the finding? No: I then found `app/worker.py` and DEPLOY_AWS.md:96-97/:211 showing the drain runs in a separate systemd process with the web box at `MEDIA_QUEUE_WORKER_ENABLED=false`, so the two copies of the module globals are guaranteed distinct rather than merely likely. (3) Does the elected-lock path (`_acquire_queue_worker_lock`, main.py:33-49) rescue it in a single-process dev setup? In dev both run in one process and the state IS shared, so the defect is production-only. (4) Does `_lock_job` prevent the double-run? Its CAS matches on `status: QUEUED`, and `recover_stale_processing_jobs` has already set the row back to QUEUED, so the CAS succeeds honestly — confirmed. Checked docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md — not recorded. Marked PARTIAL because the mechanism as written is factually wrong; LOW kept.

---

### [LOW] (backend) `plain()` was written to stop a control byte 500-ing every equality filter and was wired into exactly one of them

**Verdict:** PARTIAL

**Where.** backend/app/services/records.py:433-448 (the helper) and its single call site backend/app/api/routes/design_workshops.py:797 (`where["state"] = plain(state)`), against the unguarded equality assignments at artisans.py:237,253; products.py:96,111,113,117,119,123; processes.py:160,164,166,179; crafts.py:68-69; tools.py:102-107,117; record_filters.py:281-284,287-289.

**What it does.** `plain()` performs the same C0-control stripping as `contains()` for filters that compare equal rather than LIKE, and its docstring names `where["craftId"] = craftId` as the motivating case. A repository-wide grep for `plain(` over `app/` finds the definition at records.py:433 and one call, in design_workshops.py:797. Every `craftId`, `createdBy`, `workshopId`, `artisanId`, `productId` and equality-compared filter in the record routes and in the shared `build_record_wheres` still passes the raw parameter to Postgres, so the exact requests the docstring says it fixed still take the path it says they take. The fix was written and never applied beyond the one route that prompted it.

**How it fails.** A designer pastes an id out of an export into a filtered URL and picks up a stray control byte, or an automated client URL-encodes a null terminator: `GET /api/artisans?craftId=%00`. Starlette decodes it to a real `\x00`, `if craftId:` is truthy, the value reaches the equality comparison, Postgres cannot hold 0x00 in text, and the driver's DataError surfaces as a 500 with a stack trace in the log.

**What it costs.** Narrow — it needs a control byte in a query parameter, which no shipped client produces — but it is a logged server error on demand from any signed-in account across roughly a dozen filters, presented as an opaque server fault rather than as a bad parameter.

**Remedy.** Apply `plain()` at the equality-filter assignments the way `contains()` is applied at the LIKE ones, in artisans.py, products.py, processes.py, crafts.py, tools.py and — most importantly, because it covers /search and /map/points at once — `record_filters.build_record_wheres`. A per-route test sending a `%00` parameter and asserting 200-with-empty-result rather than 500 is the pin.

**What the verifier checked.** Verified by grep that `plain(` really has exactly one call site outside its own definition — that half of the finding is a fact, not an inference. Verified the helper's docstring names `craftId` as the motivating case, so this is a written-and-unwired fix rather than a design choice. TWO CORRECTIONS, hence PARTIAL. (1) The consequence "the web turns that into 'you are offline'" is FALSE and, again, copied from the helper's own stale docstring: `frontend/lib/offline.ts`'s `isUnreachable` deliberately excludes 5xx and `saveOrQueue:612` re-throws `ApiError`; the user sees "Something went wrong on the server". (2) Severity: the finder said MINOR; this is LOW by the brief's own rubric — it is latent, needs a byte no UI can produce, and its whole consequence is a log line and an unhelpful message. I did NOT reproduce the Postgres DataError against a live database (this is a read-only audit), so the 500 rests on the helper's own recorded measurement plus the fact that Postgres text cannot store 0x00; the unwired-fix half stands independently of that. Not in docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md, though it neighbours the open unescaped-LIKE-wildcard entry, which is about `contains` and not about `plain`.

### [LOW] (backend) Every multi-valued field accepts an unbounded array of unbounded strings — no item cap exists anywhere and `spec.max_length` is never consulted for these types

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED

**Where.** backend/app/services/stage_schema.py:968-977 (`coerce_value`, the `t.is_multi` branch, which returns before the text branch at :979-1005 where `spec.max_length` is applied), against schemas/design_workshops.py:49-50, :183-185 and :231-237

**What it does.** Quoted code is exact. The branch returns `items` with no cap on list length and no cap on any item's length; `spec.max_length` (FieldSpec, stage_schema.py:180) is consulted only in the scalar-text branch, and `FieldSpec` has no `max_items` at all. The envelope bounds count containers, not contents: `MAX_STAGE_ROWS = 500` limits `len(self.entries)` and `MAX_FIELD_KEYS = 400` limits `len(self.data)` per entry. MULTI_ENUM's allow-list does not help — duplicates of an allowed token pass the `unknown` check, so `["COTTON"] * 1_000_000` is accepted and stored. I measured the registry: 35 fields are multi-valued (IMAGE_LIST, TAGS, MULTI_ENUM) and NONE declares `max_length`, so the finder's "declared but unreachable" half is currently vacuous — the missing item cap is the whole defect. No request-size middleware exists in app/main.py.

**How it fails.** An Android build with a bug in its gallery picker, or a retry loop that appends rather than replaces, sends `stepPhotos` for one `processStep` row as an array of tens of thousands of media ids. `coerce_value` accepts every one, `_json` writes them into the `DwStageEntry.data` jsonb column, and the save answers 200. From then on every `GET /{id}/stages` — made on every stage-index open, by every client of that workshop — serialises the whole array, and `media_resolver` builds an `IN` list of every id when a report is generated.

**What it costs.** One malformed client can write an arbitrarily large blob into a JSON column that is then read back in full on the feature's most frequent read, for every designer who can see the workshop, permanently; no path trims it and no error records it. LOW because no shipped client can produce it — it needs a client bug or a hand-crafted request from an account that already has edit rights.

**Remedy.** Give `FieldSpec` a `max_items: int = 0`, enforce it in the `is_multi` branch (`if len(items) > (spec.max_items or DEFAULT_MAX_ITEMS): return None, f"{spec.label} may hold at most … entries"`) with a module-level default well above any real gallery or tag list, and apply `spec.max_length` per ITEM in the same branch so a declared bound on a multi field stops being a silent no-op. `validate_registry` should then refuse a multi field whose declared bound would never be checked, matching the rule `custom_sections._field_problems` already applies to designer-defined fields.

**What the verifier checked.** Read coerce_value's multi branch and the text branch below it; the early `return items, None` bypasses every length check. Read StageEntryIn._bound_payload and StageSaveIn's validator: both count containers. Ran the registry to count multi fields (35) and to check for a declared max_length on any of them (none), which corrects one half of the finder's claim. Grepped app/main.py for a request-size guard: only the compression middleware touches content-length. Not in docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md. LOW confirmed and not lowered further, because the write is permanent and amplified on every read.

### [LOW] (frontend) Report-history generation numbers are positions inside the truncated window, so once the 100-file export cap bites every 'Generation N' on the screen is off by the number of files cut

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED

**Where.** frontend/app/(protected)/design-workshops/[id]/report/history/page.tsx:191-195, :288, :295-299, :338, :395, :515, :739; frontend/lib/reportDiff.ts:299-305 and :343-344; backend/app/api/routes/design_workshops.py:2881, :2937-2940, :2989

**What it does.** Verified line for line. `_HISTORY_EXPORT_LIMIT = 100` (routes:2881); the query is `order={"generatedAt": "desc"}, take=_HISTORY_EXPORT_LIMIT + 1` (:2937-2940) and the response serialises `exports[:_HISTORY_EXPORT_LIMIT]` (:2989), i.e. the NEWEST hundred, with the overflow reported as `exportsTruncated`. `inGenerationOrder` (reportDiff.ts:299-305) re-sorts that window oldest-first, and both labels index into it: `generationOf` is `ordered.findIndex(...) + 1` (page:191-195), fed to `ExportCard`'s `Generation {generation}` (:338) and to the duplicate chip 'Byte-for-byte the same file as generation …' (:395); `diffExports` sets `earlierGeneration: ordered.indexOf(earlier) + 1` / `laterGeneration` (reportDiff.ts:343-344), printed as 'Generation {diff.earlierGeneration} → generation {diff.laterGeneration}' (:515). The screen discloses that files are missing (:288 'Only the most recent 100 are shown.', :739 'Only the most recent 100 files are listed.') but nowhere says the numbers are relative to the window.

**How it fails.** A workshop that regenerates on every edit exceeds 100 exports. A reviewer asks which revision introduced the corrected cost sheet; the designer quotes 'Generation 3 → generation 7' from the diff summary into a covering email, while the repository's own third file is long outside the window and an earlier visit to this same screen (before the cap bit) labelled those files differently.

**What it costs.** A screen whose stated purpose is being evidence for a ministry hands out identifiers that are neither stable nor the record's own. It costs a wrong citation rather than data, and needs more than a hundred generated files on one workshop.

**Remedy.** Either send the true position — add a `generation` (or the workshop's total export count) to `_export_payload` and index from it — or make the label honest when it cannot be: when `history.exportsTruncated` is true, render 'Generation 3 of the 100 most recent' on the card and in the diff summary, and add a line to the Limits list saying the numbering counts only the files listed.

**What the verifier checked.** I checked the two things that could have made it harmless. (1) Does the server cut from the OLD end, which would make position 1 genuinely the first file? No — `order={"generatedAt": "desc"}` with `take=limit+1` keeps the newest and drops the oldest (routes:2937-2940, :2989). (2) Does anything on screen relativise the number? The truncation is disclosed twice (:288, :739) but always separately from the label, and the duplicate chip at :395 carries the same offset (it filters only `position > 0`, which excludes undated files, not offset ones). I also confirmed the undated case is handled — `generationOf` returns 0 and `ExportCard` prints 'Undated file — it cannot be compared' (:338) — so that is not an additional defect. Severity LOW is honest and I agree with the finder's own reasoning for it.

### [LOW] (frontend) A silently-failed re-read after 'Save as the workshop's colour' repaints the whole preview in the OLD colour under a successful save

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED · found by the verifier, not the original audit

**Where.** frontend/app/(protected)/design-workshops/[id]/report/page.tsx:759-768 with :381-389 and :470-481

**What it does.** The accent save does `await refreshWorkshop(); setAccentOverride(""); if (templateId) void loadPreview(templateId);`. `refreshWorkshop` swallows every failure by design (`catch { /* the save is what mattered, and it reported itself */ }`, :386-388), so when the re-read fails the state is: the write succeeded, `settings` still holds the PREVIOUS entry, `savedAccent` (:470-475) still resolves to the previous hex, and the override — the only thing holding the new colour on screen — has just been cleared unconditionally. The comment above those two lines reasons carefully about their ORDER ('RE-READ FIRST, THEN DROP THE OVERRIDE') but not about the re-read failing, which is the one case where the order it chose is the wrong one.

**How it fails.** A designer drags in the brand colour they were handed and presses 'Save as the workshop's colour'. The PUT lands; the `GET /design-workshops/{id}` immediately after it does not (a dropped connection, a 5xx, or — on a workshop opened by its local draft id — the 404 of the first finding above, which makes this reproducible on demand). No error is shown, because the save reported success and the re-read is deliberately silent. `setAccentOverride("")` runs anyway and every sheet below repaints in the old colour.

**What it costs.** The screen shows the opposite of what happened: a save that succeeded looks like one that was discarded. The designer either saves again (harmless) or concludes the colour will not stick and picks a different one — and any download taken from that page state sends no `themeAccent` at all, so the file is built from the server's stored value while the preview beside it shows a different colour, which is precisely the preview/file disagreement this screen exists to prevent.

**Remedy.** Make the override survive a failed re-read: have `refreshWorkshop` report whether it succeeded (return a boolean, or set the detail and return it) and only `setAccentOverride("")` when the server's copy has actually been re-read; on failure keep the override, so the sheets go on showing the colour that was saved. The existing silence about the re-read is right for the settings panel and stays right here — what must not be silent is the visual revert.

---

### [LOW] (frontend) `setDraftSessionUser(null)` on sign-out can never run, because the only component that calls it lives inside the subtree AppShell stops rendering when there is no user

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED

**Where.** frontend/components/designworkshop/DraftSyncBanner.tsx:71-90; frontend/components/AppShell.tsx:40-52 and :97-105; frontend/app/(protected)/layout.tsx:44-57; frontend/components/DynamicIslandNav.tsx:372-375; frontend/lib/designWorkshopStore.ts:665-697

**What it does.** `DesignWorkshopDraftBanner` is rendered as part of `children` inside `<AppShell>` (protected layout :48-52). `AppShell` does `if (!user) return null;` at :52, before `children` is ever produced. So the instant `logout()` sets `user` to null (AuthProvider :61-68, called from DynamicIslandNav :373), the banner unmounts, and the effect at DraftSyncBanner :87-90 that would pass `null` to the store never commits — the effect has no cleanup, so nothing runs on unmount either. `sessionUserId` in designWorkshopStore.ts (:665) keeps the previous designer's id, with `sessionKnown` still true, for the whole signed-out period. The docstring at :80-84 states the opposite as a guarantee. The same holds on a route where `blocked` or `chromeHidden` is true (AppShell :97-105), where `children` is replaced by a panel and the banner is likewise not mounted.

**How it fails.** Designer A signs out on the shared district-office laptop with three unsent workshops in IndexedDB. `sessionUserId` stays 'A' and `draftBelongsToSession` (store :696) keeps answering true for A's drafts. Nothing drains today only because a repo-wide grep shows `syncDesignWorkshopDrafts` has exactly ONE call site — DraftSyncBanner.tsx:96 — which is inside the same unmounted subtree. The door is shut by there being no caller, not by the check written to shut it. Add any sync trigger outside `AppShell`'s children (a service worker, a drain in the root layout, a retry from a toast) and A's fieldwork drains under the next bearer token.

**What it costs.** Nothing observable today. The cost is that a protection the file documents as enforced is in fact load-bearing on where a component happens to be mounted — on the shared field laptop this store was built for, and on a store whose own header (:640-655) recounts the incident that made the check necessary.

**Remedy.** Move the session hand-off out of the rendered subtree: call `setDraftSessionUser(user?.id ?? null)` from an effect in `AuthProvider`, or from a small client component mounted in `app/layout.tsx` beside `AdminViewProvider` — either sits above the `!user` early return and above the route guards, so signed-out and locked-route states both reach the store. Leave the banner where it is; it should read the store, not define its session.

**What the verifier checked.** Tried hard to make this reachable and failed in the safe direction. (1) Confirmed the mount site: protected layout :50 puts `<DesignWorkshopDraftBanner />` inside `<AppShell>` as a child, and AppShell :52 returns null before rendering children when `user` is falsy. (2) Confirmed the effect has no cleanup (DraftSyncBanner :87-90), so unmount passes nothing. (3) Confirmed the sign-out path is a SOFT navigation — DynamicIslandNav :372-375 does `await logout(); router.replace("/login")` — so module state is not reset by a page load. (4) Repo-wide grep for `setDraftSessionUser` and `syncDesignWorkshopDrafts`: one caller each, both in DraftSyncBanner. That is what makes this latent rather than live, and it is why LOW is the honest severity. (5) I checked the sign-IN ordering for a worse variant — designer B signs in and opens a workshop page before the store is told: the banner precedes `{children}` in the layout tree, so its passive effect commits before the page's load effect and `ensureDraft`'s `ownerUserId: sessionUserId` stamp; no misfiling there. (6) Checked the `blocked`/`chromeHidden` branches at AppShell :97-105 — the finder is right that `children` is replaced, so the banner is absent there too, and the safe consequence is that `sessionKnown` stays false and the sync pass refuses to run. Not in docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md.

### [LOW] (frontend) `RegistrySource` declares a "memory" state nothing produces, so a tab serving a registry memoised before a deploy reports "network" and the stale-field-list banner cannot appear

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED

**Where.** frontend/lib/designWorkshopStore.ts:1606 (`export type RegistrySource = "network" | "memory" | "cache";`), :1621-1634 (loadRegistry); frontend/lib/designWorkshops.ts:769 (`let cachedRegistry: DwRegistry | null = null;`), :793, :831-832 (adoptStageRegistry seeding the same module cache); frontend/app/(protected)/design-workshops/[id]/stages/[stageKey]/page.tsx:1234 (`{registrySource === "cache" ? (`)

**What it does.** Confirmed exactly, with corrected line numbers (the finder's :1557 and :1572-1585 are stale; the code is at :1606 and :1621-1634). `grep -rn '"memory"' frontend/lib frontend/app frontend/components` returns exactly one hit, the type declaration itself — no code path produces it. `loadRegistry` returns `"network"` whenever `fetchStageRegistry()` resolves, and `fetchStageRegistry` returns the module-level `cachedRegistry` without touching the network on every call after the first in a tab's life (designWorkshops.ts:793). Nothing in the frontend ever passes `{refresh: true}` to it — the only `refresh: true` in the tree is `fetchCustomDefinition` in CustomSectionsEditor.tsx:226. The one banner that tells a designer their field list may be behind the server is gated on `registrySource === "cache"` at page.tsx:1234, which is reachable only when the network genuinely failed.

Worth adding to the finder's account: the module cache can also be seeded from the IndexedDB copy by `adoptStageRegistry` (designWorkshops.ts:831-832) on `loadRegistry`'s catch path, so a tab that started offline goes on reporting "network" for a disk copy of arbitrary age once connectivity returns. That is the same enabling condition as the singleton data loss above.

**How it fails.** A designer's laptop has had the app open since before a deploy that added a field to stage 4. They open stage 4. `fetchStageRegistry` hits the in-memory cache and returns without a request; `loadRegistry` reports `source: "network"`; the page draws the pre-deploy field list with no notice of any kind. On screen, "this stage does not ask for that" is indistinguishable from "this browser has not been told about it". The same holds, with an arbitrarily older copy, for a tab whose first registry read fell back to IndexedDB and was adopted into the module cache.

**What it costs.** The signal designed to keep those two states apart is unreachable for the commonest way a browser gets behind. On its own it costs a missing sentence; it is also the enabling condition for the singleton-key data loss above, which is why it is worth fixing.

**Remedy.** Have `fetchStageRegistry` report the provenance of its answer (or expose a `registryFetchedAt`), and have `loadRegistry` return `source: "memory"` — the state the type already declares — when the answer came off the module cache rather than the wire. Draw the same "drawn from the field list saved in this browser" note for `"memory"` as for `"cache"`, with the age. Better still, revalidate once per stage open with `{ refresh: true }` and fall back to the memoised copy on failure, which removes the staleness instead of announcing it — `fetchStageRegistry`'s refresh path already returns the previously cached OBJECT when the version is unchanged, so identity is preserved and no `useMemo` is invalidated for nothing.

**What the verifier checked.** Re-ran the grep for `"memory"` across `frontend/lib`, `frontend/app`, `frontend/components` — one hit, the type alias. Read loadRegistry (store:1621-1634) and confirmed only two of the three variants are returned. Read fetchStageRegistry (designWorkshops.ts:792-816) and confirmed the early return at :793. Grepped the whole frontend for `refresh: true` / `refresh:true` to see whether any caller revalidates — only the custom-definition fetch does. Read page.tsx:1234 and confirmed the banner's gate is `=== "cache"` alone. Held at LOW: the union member being unreachable costs only a missing sentence by itself, the window closes on any full page reload, and the state cannot be produced deterministically.

### [LOW] (android) A readiness link to a designer's own required custom question opens the stage with nothing scrolled to or highlighted, because the Android stage form draws no section under the entity key the address names

**Verdict:** PARTIAL

**Where.** android/app/src/main/java/com/designprototype/workshop/data/DwSubmissionReadiness.kt:404-423 (address built at :411, comment at :395-397); android/app/src/main/java/com/designprototype/workshop/ui/designworkshop/StageScreen.kt:1282, :1305-1317, :1325; android/app/src/main/java/com/designprototype/workshop/ui/designworkshop/DwCustomSectionForm.kt:62-101; android/app/src/main/java/com/designprototype/workshop/data/DwCustomSections.kt:492

**What it does.** `stageAddresses` files a custom field under `entityKey = customSectionEntityKey(block.section)` (DwSubmissionReadiness.kt:411), i.e. `"_custom:<sectionKey>"`. `StageScreen` routes `focus` to exactly two places: the singleton, `focusFieldKey = focus?.takeIf { it.entityKey == entity.key && it.rowKey == null }?.fieldKey` (:1282), and each collection, `focus = focus?.takeIf { it.entityKey == entity.key }` (:1325). The custom form is called with no focus at all (:1305-1317) and `DwCustomSectionForm`/`DwCustomSectionBlock`/`DwCustomFieldRow` take no focus parameter and carry no `BringIntoViewRequester` or border anchor (DwCustomSectionForm.kt:62-157) — the anchoring machinery lives only in `EntitySection` (StageScreen.kt:1603-1624). `focusOpensAdvanced` cannot match it either, since it tests `stage.singleton` (:2020-2024). So the address is unusable by this client, unconditionally.

**How it fails.** A designer adds "How many looms are in working order?" to stage 5 as a required question on the web. The handset's stage index lists it as blocking and `addressBook` gives the row a focus. The designer taps it, lands on stage 5 — singleton, "More detail" disclosure, custom block, three collections — and nothing scrolls, nothing is outlined, and no accessibility focus moves. They find their own question by eye.

**What it costs.** A deep link that degrades to "open the stage" for the one blocking item a designer is most likely to have. No data is wrong and nothing is lost; `DwSubmissionReadiness` itself documents this degrade as acceptable ("landing on the right form with the box unhighlighted is a far smaller failure", :218-225). What is actually incorrect is the comment at :395-397 asserting `customSectionEntityKey` is "the string the stage form draws the section under" — true of the web, false of this form.

**Remedy.** Fix it on the FORM side only. Add `focus: DwStageFocus?` to `DwCustomSectionForm`, pass `focus?.takeIf { it.entityKey.startsWith("$CUSTOM_ENTITY_KEY:") && it.rowKey == null }` from StageScreen.kt:1305, and in `DwCustomSectionBlock` match `focus.entityKey == customSectionEntityKey(section)` to apply the same requester/border anchor `EntitySection` uses. Do NOT take the finder's fallback of filing the address under bare `CUSTOM_ENTITY_KEY`: the address is deliberately the web's, and `stageFieldHref` is required to produce the browser's URL character for character (DwSubmissionReadiness.kt:190-198) — the web's stage form really does render the section as an entity keyed `_custom:<key>` (frontend/lib/customSections.ts:537-543, frontend/components/designworkshop/CustomSections.tsx:146), so changing it would break the shared link and the browser's own focus. If the form side is not wanted, correct the comment at :395-397 instead.

**What the verifier checked.** Verified the address construction at DwSubmissionReadiness.kt:404-423 and `customSectionEntityKey` at DwCustomSections.kt:492; grepped every `customSectionEntityKey` caller in android/app/src (two in main, one in a test) and every `focus` reference in StageScreen.kt — the two `takeIf` sites at :1282 and :1325 are the only consumers. Read DwCustomSectionForm.kt end to end to confirm no focus parameter and no anchor. I then checked whether the address is simply wrong, which would have been the cheaper fix, and it is not: frontend/lib/submissionReadiness.ts:227 builds the identical key and the web renders under `customSectionEntity(section).key`, so the string is correct and the Android form is the half that never landed. Downgraded MINOR→LOW: no data consequence, the module explicitly builds this degrade in, and the item still appears and is still tappable.
